Now, in glycoside hydrolases, particularly those in the retaining mechanism (like GH family enzymes), the catalytic mechanism typically involves two glutamate residues:
1. One acts as the nucleophile (attacks the glycosidic bond)

In the classical retaining glycoside hydrolase mechanism:
- The nucleophile is typically located closer to the N-terminus (in the (β/α)8 TIM barrel fold)
- The general acid/base (proton donor) is typically located further downstream

In a retaining mechanism:
- Nucleophile: directly attacks the anomeric carbon
- General acid (proton donor): protonates the leaving group during the first step
- General base: deprotonates water during the second step (the same residue that acted as acid now acts as base)

So in a retaining GH, the two Glu residues are:
- Nucleophile (first Glu, closer to N-term in most families)
- General acid/base = proton donor (second Glu)

For inverting GH:
- General base: deprotonates the water nucleophile
- General acid (proton donor): protonates the leaving group
